========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello Doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, I just don't have the push I used to have anymore. I used to be so busy with the church choir and my quilting, and visiting folks down the road. 

I used to make these big star-pattern quilts, you know. My mother taught me how to piece them together when I was just a little girl, back before I had to leave school to help out at the house. We had this old Singer machine with the foot pedal... it was heavy iron, just beautiful. You don't see machines like that now, everything is cheap plastic and breaks if you look at it wrong. 

But... what was I saying? Oh, right. Most days now, I just sit at home. I leave the television on real low. I like watching those old game shows. Bob Barker was always such a nice-looking man in those suits, wasn't he? He always treated the ladies so polite. I know people are expecting me, the choir director keeps calling to ask where I am, but I just sit there in my chair with the TV on.

Forgetting things? No, I don't think so. My memory is still pretty sharp, thank the Lord. Like I said, I remember perfectly well when the choir director called. She was talking about the bake sale they're having next month. 

I used to bake a peach cobbler for that every single year. My sister Martha had this big old peach tree right in her backyard, and we'd go out with these woven wicker baskets to pick them. The peaches back then were just so sweet, the juice would run right down your chin. Not like the hard ones you get at the grocery store now, sitting in those plastic boxes. They don't even smell like peaches anymore, do they? Martha's yard always smelled so sweet in the late summer... she had big bushes of honeysuckle all along the fence, too. The little hummingbirds just loved that honeysuckle. We'd sit on her porch swing and just watch them zip around... it squeaked a little, that swing, but it was a nice sound.

Sad? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around crying or feeling sorry for myself, if that's what you mean. I just... sit. It's just quiet. 

I used to love the quiet, actually. When I was a girl, early mornings before the sun came up, that was the best time. The whole house was still asleep, and I'd go out to the porch with a cup of hot water and lemon. My grandmother swore by hot water and lemon for your digestion. She lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. She had this beautiful porcelain teacup with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I think my cousin Sarah got that cup when she passed. Sarah always did have a way of getting the nice things. She married a man who sold insurance, made a very good living. They bought a house with a wrap-around porch, just beautiful. But no, I don't feel sad. Just... not much of anything, really.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, managing tasks... I suppose I have had to step back from some things. I used to be right up there leading the choir, you know, and helping out with all the projects down at the senior center. But lately, I just don't keep up with what I promised to do. The church friends have started checking on me a lot more than they used to... dropping by with casseroles and such. 

Mary brought by a green bean casserole just last Tuesday. She uses those little crispy fried onions on top, from the can. I always preferred making them from scratch, though. You just slice the onions real thin, dredge them in a little flour and buttermilk, and fry them up in a cast iron skillet. My mother had this heavy skillet, black as coal, must have weighed ten pounds. She cooked everything in it—fried chicken, cornbread, even pineapple upside-down cake. The sugar would caramelize right against the iron, oh, it was just heavenly. We'd have it on Sundays after church, sitting out on the picnic table under the big oak tree. The squirrels used to come right down the trunk, trying to get a crumb... cheeky little things, they were. But yes, I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my projects like I used to.

She had this beautiful porcelain teacup with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I think my cousin Sarah got that cup when she passed. Sarah always did have a way of getting the nice things. She married a man who sold insurance, made a very good living. They bought a house with a wrap-around porch, just beautiful. But no, I don't feel sad. Just... not much of anything, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new difficulty managing everyday tasks—like following your quilting patterns, handling your finances, or keeping track of household chores—that used to come easily to you?\n\nPatient: Well, managing tasks... I suppose I have had to step back from some things. I used to be right up there leading the choir, you know, and helping out with all the projects down at the senior center. But lately, I just don't keep up with what I promised to do. The church friends have started checking on me a lot more than they used to... dropping by with casseroles and such. \n\nMary brought by a green bean casserole just last Tuesday. She uses those little crispy fried onions on top, from the can. I always preferred making them from scratch, though. You just slice the onions real thin, dredge them in a little flour and buttermilk, and fry them up in a cast iron skillet. My mother had this heavy skillet, black as coal, must have weighed ten pounds. She cooked everything in it—fried chicken, cornbread, even pineapple upside-down cake. The sugar would caramelize right against the iron, oh, it was just heavenly. We'd have it on Sundays after church, sitting out on the picnic table under the big oak tree. The squirrels used to come right down the trunk, trying to get a crumb... cheeky little things, they were. But yes, I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my projects like I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nThinking back, did this drop in energy and interest come on fairly suddenly over a few weeks, or has it been creeping up slowly over a year or more?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it definitely wasn't sudden. It's been building up slow, ever since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to help out in the front, you know, answering the phones and keeping the files straight. 

The children were always coming in with notes from their mothers, or needing a bandage for a scraped knee from the playground. We kept a big tin of those bandages right on the counter... the ones with the little cartoon characters on them. The kids just loved them. I remember one little boy, Tommy, he'd come in almost every Tuesday just to get a bandage with a green dinosaur on it. He was a sweet little thing. His mother worked down at the hardware store, the one on Main Street that closed down last winter. It really is a shame it closed, they always had the absolute best prices on potting soil. The owner, Mr. Henderson, would even carry the bags out to my car for me. I used to buy big heavy bags of it for my geraniums. Geraniums need good drainage, you know, otherwise the roots just rot right out from under them. I had bright red ones in these terracotta pots all along the front steps...

But yes, I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my projects like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did this drop in energy and interest come on fairly suddenly over a few weeks, or has it been creeping up slowly over a year or more?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely wasn't sudden. It's been building up slow, ever since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to help out in the front, you know, answering the phones and keeping the files straight. \n\nThe children were always coming in with notes from their mothers, or needing a bandage for a scraped knee from the playground. We kept a big tin of those bandages right on the counter... the ones with the little cartoon characters on them. The kids just loved them. I remember one little boy, Tommy, he'd come in almost every Tuesday just to get a bandage with a green dinosaur on it. He was a sweet little thing. His mother worked down at the hardware store, the one on Main Street that closed down last winter. It really is a shame it closed, they always had the absolute best prices on potting soil. The owner, Mr. Henderson, would even carry the bags out to my car for me. I used to buy big heavy bags of it for my geraniums. Geraniums need good drainage, you know, otherwise the roots just rot right out from under them. I had bright red ones in these terracotta pots all along the front steps...\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you or anyone else noticed you getting confused about the day or date, or losing your way in places you know well, like your own neighborhood?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Confused about the day? Well, no, not really. I mean, I know it's Thursday because the trash trucks came by this morning. They always make such a racket, clanging those big metal bins around. 

The driver, he's a big fellow, always wears this bright orange vest. It reminds me of the hunting vests my husband used to wear when he'd go up to the cabin with his brothers. They'd pack up the truck with coolers full of sandwiches and beer, and stay up there for the whole weekend. I never did care for the taste of venison, though. It always seemed too gamey to me. My sister-in-law, Martha, she had a recipe for venison stew that she swore by. She'd put in parsnips and carrots and let it simmer all day in the slow cooker. Martha always had the nicest kitchen gadgets. She bought one of those fancy standing mixers, the ones that come in all the pretty colors. Hers was a pale yellow, like butter. I always thought if I got one, I'd get the cherry red. It just looks so cheerful sitting on the counter. But no, I haven't been getting lost or anything like that.

The church friends have started checking on me a lot more than they used to... dropping by with casseroles and such. \n\nMary brought by a green bean casserole just last Tuesday. She uses those little crispy fried onions on top, from the can. I always preferred making them from scratch, though. You just slice the onions real thin, dredge them in a little flour and buttermilk, and fry them up in a cast iron skillet. My mother had this heavy skillet, black as coal, must have weighed ten pounds. She cooked everything in it—fried chicken, cornbread, even pineapple upside-down cake. The sugar would caramelize right against the iron, oh, it was just heavenly. We'd have it on Sundays after church, sitting out on the picnic table under the big oak tree. The squirrels used to come right down the trunk, trying to get a crumb... cheeky little things, they were. But yes, I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my projects like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did this drop in energy and interest come on fairly suddenly over a few weeks, or has it been creeping up slowly over a year or more?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely wasn't sudden. It's been building up slow, ever since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to help out in the front, you know, answering the phones and keeping the files straight. \n\nThe children were always coming in with notes from their mothers, or needing a bandage for a scraped knee from the playground. We kept a big tin of those bandages right on the counter... the ones with the little cartoon characters on them. The kids just loved them. I remember one little boy, Tommy, he'd come in almost every Tuesday just to get a bandage with a green dinosaur on it. He was a sweet little thing. His mother worked down at the hardware store, the one on Main Street that closed down last winter. It really is a shame it closed, they always had the absolute best prices on potting soil. The owner, Mr. Henderson, would even carry the bags out to my car for me. I used to buy big heavy bags of it for my geraniums. Geraniums need good drainage, you know, otherwise the roots just rot right out from under them. I had bright red ones in these terracotta pots all along the front steps...\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone else noticed you getting confused about the day or date, or losing your way in places you know well, like your own neighborhood?\n\nPatient: Confused about the day? Well, no, not really. I mean, I know it's Thursday because the trash trucks came by this morning. They always make such a racket, clanging those big metal bins around. \n\nThe driver, he's a big fellow, always wears this bright orange vest. It reminds me of the hunting vests my husband used to wear when he'd go up to the cabin with his brothers. They'd pack up the truck with coolers full of sandwiches and beer, and stay up there for the whole weekend. I never did care for the taste of venison, though. It always seemed too gamey to me. My sister-in-law, Martha, she had a recipe for venison stew that she swore by. She'd put in parsnips and carrots and let it simmer all day in the slow cooker. Martha always had the nicest kitchen gadgets. She bought one of those fancy standing mixers, the ones that come in all the pretty colors. Hers was a pale yellow, like butter. I always thought if I got one, I'd get the cherry red. It just looks so cheerful sitting on the counter. But no, I haven't been getting lost or anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your family or friends mentioned that you've been repeating the same questions or stories to them without realizing you'd already said them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Repeating stories? No, I don't think my friends have said anything like that. Well, I suppose Mary did tell me I already mentioned the new curtains I was making for the spare room. 

I found the most beautiful floral print fabric down at the remnant store. It has these tiny little blue cornflowers all over it, with just a hint of yellow in the center. Cornflowers always remind me of my grandmother's garden. She had a whole patch of them right next to the rhubarb. She used to make the most tart rhubarb pie... you'd have to put a huge scoop of vanilla ice cream right on top just to cut the sourness. We used to get the ice cream in those big cardboard tubs from the dairy down the road. They had a big fiberglass cow out front, painted black and white. The paint was always peeling on its nose because all the kids would climb up and sit on it while their parents bought milk. I wonder if that cow is still there... they built a whole new shopping center out that way a few years back. But no, I don't think I ask the same questions over and over.

My sister-in-law, Martha, she had a recipe for venison stew that she swore by. She'd put in parsnips and carrots and let it simmer all day in the slow cooker. Martha always had the nicest kitchen gadgets. She bought one of those fancy standing mixers, the ones that come in all the pretty colors. Hers was a pale yellow, like butter. I always thought if I got one, I'd get the cherry red. It just looks so cheerful sitting on the counter. But no, I haven't been getting lost or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your family or friends mentioned that you've been repeating the same questions or stories to them without realizing you'd already said them?\n\nPatient: Repeating stories? No, I don't think my friends have said anything like that. Well, I suppose Mary did tell me I already mentioned the new curtains I was making for the spare room. \n\nI found the most beautiful floral print fabric down at the remnant store. It has these tiny little blue cornflowers all over it, with just a hint of yellow in the center. Cornflowers always remind me of my grandmother's garden. She had a whole patch of them right next to the rhubarb. She used to make the most tart rhubarb pie... you'd have to put a huge scoop of vanilla ice cream right on top just to cut the sourness. We used to get the ice cream in those big cardboard tubs from the dairy down the road. They had a big fiberglass cow out front, painted black and white. The paint was always peeling on its nose because all the kids would climb up and sit on it while their parents bought milk. I wonder if that cow is still there... they built a whole new shopping center out that way a few years back. But no, I don't think I ask the same questions over and over.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your family or friends mentioned any changes in your personality or behavior lately—like seeming less concerned about things that used to matter to you, or acting in ways that seem out of character for you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Out of character? Well, I suppose the ladies down at the senior center have been looking at me a bit funny lately. They said... well, they mentioned I do some peculiar things sometimes. Like I'll just get right up in the middle of our time and start moving all the chairs around into rows, you know, setting them up just like a school classroom. 

We used to have those heavy wooden desks at the school I worked at, the ones with the little groove at the top so your pencil wouldn't roll off. The janitor, Mr. Higgins, he'd polish them every single summer with this wax that smelled just like fresh lemons. I always loved the smell of lemons. I still buy those hard lemon drops at the pharmacy... the ones covered in powdered sugar that come in the little round tin. You have to pry the lid off with a butter knife sometimes because the sugar gets all stuck in the grooves. 

But anyway, the ladies also say they'll catch me just folding the exact same quilt square over and over and over again. They'll be asking me to stop, saying I'm wrinkling the fabric, but I guess I just keep at it. And afterward, when they ask me about it, I can't rightly explain what I was doing or why I was doing it. I just don't know. We're making a morning star pattern quilt right now, actually. My grandmother used to make those... she'd use old feed sacks for the backing because they didn't have much money. You wouldn't believe it, but they printed the prettiest floral patterns on those feed sacks back then, just so the women could use the fabric for sewing.

No, I don't think so. My memory is still pretty sharp, thank the Lord. Like I said, I remember perfectly well when the choir director called. She was talking about the bake sale they're having next month. \n\nI used to bake a peach cobbler for that every single year. My sister Martha had this big old peach tree right in her backyard, and we'd go out with these woven wicker baskets to pick them. Hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around crying or feeling sorry for myself, if that's what you mean. I just... sit. It's just quiet. \n\nI used to love the quiet, actually. When I was a girl, early mornings before the sun came up, that was the best time. The whole house was still asleep, and I'd go out to the porch with a cup of hot water and lemon. My grandmother swore by hot water and lemon for your digestion. She lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. She had this beautiful porcelain teacup with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I think my cousin Sarah got that cup when she passed. Sarah always did have a way of getting the nice things. Have the ladies at the center noticed me sitting still? Oh, like freezing up or not talking? No, they haven't mentioned anything like that. I don't think I do that. We talk plenty at the center.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Blunt or inappropriate? Oh, no, I don't think so. I've always prided myself on having good manners. My mother was a stickler for manners, you see. 

She used to make us sit at the dining table with books balanced on our heads to practice our posture. If the book fell, you didn't get dessert. And dessert was always something special on Sundays, usually a tapioca pudding. I never really liked tapioca... the texture always reminded me of frog eggs. We used to go down to the creek behind the house in the spring and collect frog eggs in these big glass mason jars. The jars had those heavy zinc lids with the white glass liners. My grandmother used them for canning peaches. She'd spend the whole month of August just peeling and boiling peaches in this massive copper kettle she kept out on the back porch. The whole house would smell like sweet summer peaches for weeks. You just can't find peaches like that at the supermarket anymore, they're always so hard and mealy. I tried to make a peach cobbler last week, but the crust just didn't turn out right. I think my baking powder might be too old. But no, I haven't been saying anything rude or impulsive. I always mind my p's and q's.

You just slice the onions real thin, dredge them in a little flour and buttermilk, and fry them up in a cast iron skillet. The sugar would caramelize right against the iron, oh, it was just heavenly. We'd have it on Sundays after church, sitting out on the picnic table under the big oak tree. It's been building up slow, ever since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I remember one little boy, Tommy, he'd come in almost every Tuesday just to get a bandage with a green dinosaur on it. He was a sweet little thing. His mother worked down at the hardware store, the one on Main Street that closed down last winter. It really is a shame it closed, they always had the absolute best prices on potting soil. The owner, Mr. Henderson, would even carry the bags out to my car for me. I used to buy big heavy bags of it for my geraniums. Geraniums need good drainage, you know, otherwise the roots just rot right out from under them. I had bright red ones in these terracotta pots all along the front steps...\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone else noticed you getting confused about the day or date, or losing your way in places you know well, like your own neighborhood?\n\nPatient: Confused about the day? Well, no, not really. I mean, I know it's Thursday because the trash trucks came by this morning. They always make such a racket, clanging those big metal bins around. \n\nThe driver, he's a big fellow, always wears this bright orange vest. It reminds me of the hunting vests my husband used to wear when he'd go up to the cabin with his brothers. They'd pack up the truck with coolers full of sandwiches and beer, and stay up there for the whole weekend. I never did care for the taste of venison, though. It always seemed too gamey to me. My sister-in-law, Martha, she had a recipe for venison stew that she swore by. She'd put in parsnips and carrots and let it simmer all day in the slow cooker. Martha always had the nicest kitchen gadgets. She bought one of those fancy standing mixers, the ones that come in all the pretty colors. Hers was a pale yellow, like butter. I always thought if I got one, I'd get the cherry red. It just looks so cheerful sitting on the counter. But no, I haven't been getting lost or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your family or friends mentioned that you've been repeating the same questions or stories to them without realizing you'd already said them?\n\nPatient: Repeating stories? Like I'll just get right up in the middle of our time and start moving all the chairs around into rows, you know, setting them up just like a school classroom. \n\nWe used to have those heavy wooden desks at the school I worked at, the ones with the little groove at the top so your pencil wouldn't roll off. The janitor, Mr. Higgins, he'd polish them every single summer with this wax that smelled just like fresh lemons. I always loved the smell of lemons. I still buy those hard lemon drops at the pharmacy... the ones covered in powdered sugar that come in the little round tin. You have to pry the lid off with a butter knife sometimes because the sugar gets all stuck in the grooves. \n\nBut anyway, the ladies also say they'll catch me just folding the exact same quilt square over and over and over again. They'll be asking me to stop, saying I'm wrinkling the fabric, but I guess I just keep at it. And afterward, when they ask me about it, I can't rightly explain what I was doing or why I was doing it. The blueberries are so big and plump, not like those little shriveled things you get in the frozen aisle. My mother used to grow blueberries in the backyard. She had these huge bushes that would just hang down with the weight of them. We'd go out with these little metal pails—the kind you take to the beach to build sandcastles—and pick them until our fingers were stained purple. I always wanted a red pail, but my brother took the red one, so I had to use the blue one. He was always taking my things. He took my favorite doll once and hid it up in the hayloft. I cried for three days until my father finally climbed up there and found it. But no, I don't just sit there frozen or quiet. I'm always chatting or doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have your friends or family noticed you saying or doing things lately that seem unusually blunt, impulsive, or socially inappropriate for you—things you wouldn't have said or done in the past?\n\nPatient: Blunt or inappropriate? Oh, no, I don't think so. I've always prided myself on having good manners. My mother was a stickler for manners, you see. \n\nShe used to make us sit at the dining table with books balanced on our heads to practice our posture. If the book fell, you didn't get dessert. And dessert was always something special on Sundays, usually a tapioca pudding. I never really liked tapioca... the texture always reminded me of frog eggs. We used to go down to the creek behind the house in the spring and collect frog eggs in these big glass mason jars. The jars had those heavy zinc lids with the white glass liners. My grandmother used them for canning peaches. She'd spend the whole month of August just peeling and boiling peaches in this massive copper kettle she kept out on the back porch. The whole house would smell like sweet summer peaches for weeks. You just can't find peaches like that at the supermarket anymore, they're always so hard and mealy. I tried to make a peach cobbler last week, but the crust just didn't turn out right. I think my baking powder might be too old. But no, I haven't been saying anything rude or impulsive. I always mind my p's and q's.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you or your family noticed any new tremors, stiffness, slowed movements, or trouble with your walking or balance recently?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Trouble with my walking? Oh, no, my balance is just fine. I don't have any shaking or stiffness, nothing like that. I still walk down to the corner market every Tuesday morning to get the paper. I like to check the grocery ads, you see. Though the prices these days... well, I remember when you could get a whole pound of coffee for under a dollar. 

My father drank his coffee black as tar. He boiled it right on the stove in this speckled blue enamel pot. It had a little chip on the spout where he knocked it against the sink once. He kept the coffee grounds in a big red tin, and when it was empty, he'd use the tins to hold nails and screws out in the shed. That shed always smelled like motor oil and damp earth, especially after a good rain. We had a family of raccoons get under the floorboards out there one winter. They make the strangest chittering noises, you know, almost like little birds. My brother tried to coax them out with pieces of crusty bread, but they just snatched the bread right out of his hand and scurried back under the wood. He was so mad about that bread.

I know people are expecting me, the choir director keeps calling to ask where I am, but I just sit there in my chair with the TV on.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed you forgetting recent things, like appointments or conversations you had earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Forgetting things? No, I don't think so. My memory is still pretty sharp, thank the Lord. Like I said, I remember perfectly well when the choir director called. She was talking about the bake sale they're having next month. \n\nI used to bake a peach cobbler for that every single year. My sister Martha had this big old peach tree right in her backyard, and we'd go out with these woven wicker baskets to pick them. The peaches back then were just so sweet, the juice would run right down your chin. Not like the hard ones you get at the grocery store now, sitting in those plastic boxes. They don't even smell like peaches anymore, do they? Martha's yard always smelled so sweet in the late summer... she had big bushes of honeysuckle all along the fence, too. Hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around crying or feeling sorry for myself, if that's what you mean. I just... sit. It's just quiet. \n\nI used to love the quiet, actually. When I was a girl, early mornings before the sun came up, that was the best time. The whole house was still asleep, and I'd go out to the porch with a cup of hot water and lemon. My grandmother swore by hot water and lemon for your digestion. She lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. She had this beautiful porcelain teacup with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I think my cousin Sarah got that cup when she passed. Sarah always did have a way of getting the nice things. She married a man who sold insurance, made a very good living. They bought a house with a wrap-around porch, just beautiful. But no, I don't feel sad. You just slice the onions real thin, dredge them in a little flour and buttermilk, and fry them up in a cast iron skillet. My mother had this heavy skillet, black as coal, must have weighed ten pounds. She cooked everything in it—fried chicken, cornbread, even pineapple upside-down cake. The sugar would caramelize right against the iron, oh, it was just heavenly. We'd have it on Sundays after church, sitting out on the picnic table under the big oak tree. The squirrels used to come right down the trunk, trying to get a crumb... cheeky little things, they were. We kept a big tin of those bandages right on the counter... the ones with the little cartoon characters on them. The kids just loved them. I remember one little boy, Tommy, he'd come in almost every Tuesday just to get a bandage with a green dinosaur on it. He was a sweet little thing. His mother worked down at the hardware store, the one on Main Street that closed down last winter. It really is a shame it closed, they always had the absolute best prices on potting soil. The owner, Mr. Henderson, would even carry the bags out to my car for me. I used to buy big heavy bags of it for my geraniums. Geraniums need good drainage, you know, otherwise the roots just rot right out from under them. I had bright red ones in these terracotta pots all along the front steps...\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone else noticed you getting confused about the day or date, or losing your way in places you know well, like your own neighborhood?\n\nPatient: Confused about the day? Well, no, not really. I mean, I know it's Thursday because the trash trucks came by this morning. It reminds me of the hunting vests my husband used to wear when he'd go up to the cabin with his brothers. They'd pack up the truck with coolers full of sandwiches and beer, and stay up there for the whole weekend. I never did care for the taste of venison, though. It always seemed too gamey to me. My sister-in-law, Martha, she had a recipe for venison stew that she swore by. She'd put in parsnips and carrots and let it simmer all day in the slow cooker. Martha always had the nicest kitchen gadgets. She bought one of those fancy standing mixers, the ones that come in all the pretty colors. Hers was a pale yellow, like butter. I always thought if I got one, I'd get the cherry red. It just looks so cheerful sitting on the counter. She had a whole patch of them right next to the rhubarb. She used to make the most tart rhubarb pie... you'd have to put a huge scoop of vanilla ice cream right on top just to cut the sourness. We used to get the ice cream in those big cardboard tubs from the dairy down the road. They had a big fiberglass cow out front, painted black and white. The paint was always peeling on its nose because all the kids would climb up and sit on it while their parents bought milk. She was talking about the bake sale they're having next month. \n\nI used to bake a peach cobbler for that every single year. My sister Martha had this big old peach tree right in her backyard, and we'd go out with these woven wicker baskets to pick them. The peaches back then were just so sweet, the juice would run right down your chin. Not like the hard ones you get at the grocery store now, sitting in those plastic boxes. They don't even smell like peaches anymore, do they? Martha's yard always smelled so sweet in the late summer... she had big bushes of honeysuckle all along the fence, too. The little hummingbirds just loved that honeysuckle. We'd sit on her porch swing and just watch them zip around... it squeaked a little, that swing, but it was a nice sound.\n\nDoctor: Aside from having less energy or motivation, have you been feeling sad, down, or hopeless most days?\n\nPatient: Sad? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around crying or feeling sorry for myself, if that's what you mean. I just... sit. It's just quiet. \n\nI used to love the quiet, actually. When I was a girl, early mornings before the sun came up, that was the best time. The whole house was still asleep, and I'd go out to the porch with a cup of hot water and lemon. My grandmother swore by hot water and lemon for your digestion. She lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. She had this beautiful porcelain teacup with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I think my cousin Sarah got that cup when she passed. Sarah always did have a way of getting the nice things. She married a man who sold insurance, made a very good living. They bought a house with a wrap-around porch, just beautiful. But no, I don't feel sad. Just... not much of anything, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new difficulty managing everyday tasks—like following your quilting patterns, handling your finances, or keeping track of household chores—that used to come easily to you?\n\nPatient: Well, managing tasks... I suppose I have had to step back from some things. I used to be right up there leading the choir, you know, and helping out with all the projects down at the senior center. But lately, I just don't keep up with what I promised to do. The church friends have started checking on me a lot more than they used to... dropping by with casseroles and such. \n\nMary brought by a green bean casserole just last Tuesday. She uses those little crispy fried onions on top, from the can. I always preferred making them from scratch, though. You just slice the onions real thin, dredge them in a little flour and buttermilk, and fry them up in a cast iron skillet. My mother had this heavy skillet, black as coal, must have weighed ten pounds. She cooked everything in it—fried chicken, cornbread, even pineapple upside-down cake. The sugar would caramelize right against the iron, oh, it was just heavenly. We'd have it on Sundays after church, sitting out on the picnic table under the big oak tree. The squirrels used to come right down the trunk, trying to get a crumb... cheeky little things, they were. But yes, I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my projects like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did this drop in energy and interest come on fairly suddenly over a few weeks, or has it been creeping up slowly over a year or more?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely wasn't sudden. It's been building up slow, ever since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to help out in the front, you know, answering the phones and keeping the files straight. \n\nThe children were always coming in with notes from their mothers, or needing a bandage for a scraped knee from the playground. We kept a big tin of those bandages right on the counter... the ones with the little cartoon characters on them. The kids just loved them. I remember one little boy, Tommy, he'd come in almost every Tuesday just to get a bandage with a green dinosaur on it. He was a sweet little thing. His mother worked down at the hardware store, the one on Main Street that closed down last winter. It really is a shame it closed, they always had the absolute best prices on potting soil. The owner, Mr. Henderson, would even carry the bags out to my car for me. I used to buy big heavy bags of it for my geraniums. Geraniums need good drainage, you know, otherwise the roots just rot right out from under them. I had bright red ones in these terracotta pots all along the front steps...\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone else noticed you getting confused about the day or date, or losing your way in places you know well, like your own neighborhood?\n\nPatient: Confused about the day? Well, no, not really. I mean, I know it's Thursday because the trash trucks came by this morning. They always make such a racket, clanging those big metal bins around. \n\nThe driver, he's a big fellow, always wears this bright orange vest. It reminds me of the hunting vests my husband used to wear when he'd go up to the cabin with his brothers. They'd pack up the truck with coolers full of sandwiches and beer, and stay up there for the whole weekend. I never did care for the taste of venison, though. It always seemed too gamey to me. My sister-in-law, Martha, she had a recipe for venison stew that she swore by. She'd put in parsnips and carrots and let it simmer all day in the slow cooker. Martha always had the nicest kitchen gadgets. She bought one of those fancy standing mixers, the ones that come in all the pretty colors. Hers was a pale yellow, like butter. I always thought if I got one, I'd get the cherry red. It just looks so cheerful sitting on the counter. But no, I haven't been getting lost or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your family or friends mentioned that you've been repeating the same questions or stories to them without realizing you'd already said them?\n\nPatient: Repeating stories? No, I don't think my friends have said anything like that. Well, I suppose Mary did tell me I already mentioned the new curtains I was making for the spare room. \n\nI found the most beautiful floral print fabric down at the remnant store. It has these tiny little blue cornflowers all over it, with just a hint of yellow in the center. Cornflowers always remind me of my grandmother's garden. She had a whole patch of them right next to the rhubarb. She used to make the most tart rhubarb pie... you'd have to put a huge scoop of vanilla ice cream right on top just to cut the sourness. We used to get the ice cream in those big cardboard tubs from the dairy down the road. They had a big fiberglass cow out front, painted black and white. The paint was always peeling on its nose because all the kids would climb up and sit on it while their parents bought milk. I wonder if that cow is still there... they built a whole new shopping center out that way a few years back. But no, I don't think I ask the same questions over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have your family or friends mentioned any changes in your personality or behavior lately—like seeming less concerned about things that used to matter to you, or acting in ways that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Out of character? Well, I suppose the ladies down at the senior center have been looking at me a bit funny lately. They said... well, they mentioned I do some peculiar things sometimes. Like I'll just get right up in the middle of our time and start moving all the chairs around into rows, you know, setting them up just like a school classroom. \n\nWe used to have those heavy wooden desks at the school I worked at, the ones with the little groove at the top so your pencil wouldn't roll off. The janitor, Mr. Higgins, he'd polish them every single summer with this wax that smelled just like fresh lemons. I always loved the smell of lemons. I still buy those hard lemon drops at the pharmacy... the ones covered in powdered sugar that come in the little round tin. You have to pry the lid off with a butter knife sometimes because the sugar gets all stuck in the grooves. \n\nBut anyway, the ladies also say they'll catch me just folding the exact same quilt square over and over and over again. They'll be asking me to stop, saying I'm wrinkling the fabric, but I guess I just keep at it. And afterward, when they ask me about it, I can't rightly explain what I was doing or why I was doing it. I just don't know. We're making a morning star pattern quilt right now, actually. My grandmother used to make those... she'd use old feed sacks for the backing because they didn't have much money. You wouldn't believe it, but they printed the prettiest floral patterns on those feed sacks back then, just so the women could use the fabric for sewing.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for catatonic signs (e.g., prolonged immobility, mutism, waxy flexibility, or resistance to movement) to differentiate catatonia from a primary behavioral-variant dementia process\", \"question\": \"Have the ladies at the center\n\nPatient: Have the ladies at the center noticed me sitting still? Oh, like freezing up or not talking? No, they haven't mentioned anything like that. I don't think I do that. We talk plenty at the center. \n\nWe had this lovely chat yesterday about the new bakery that opened up on Main Street. Have you been there? They make these wonderful blueberry scones. The blueberries are so big and plump, not like those little shriveled things you get in the frozen aisle. My mother used to grow blueberries in the backyard. She had these huge bushes that would just hang down with the weight of them. We'd go out with these little metal pails—the kind you take to the beach to build sandcastles—and pick them until our fingers were stained purple. I always wanted a red pail, but my brother took the red one, so I had to use the blue one. He was always taking my things. He took my favorite doll once and hid it up in the hayloft. I cried for three days until my father finally climbed up there and found it. But no, I don't just sit there frozen or quiet. Oh, no, I don't think so. I've always prided myself on having good manners. My mother was a stickler for manners, you see. \n\nShe used to make us sit at the dining table with books balanced on our heads to practice our posture. If the book fell, you didn't get dessert. And dessert was always something special on Sundays, usually a tapioca pudding. I never really liked tapioca... the texture always reminded me of frog eggs. We used to go down to the creek behind the house in the spring and collect frog eggs in these big glass mason jars. The jars had those heavy zinc lids with the white glass liners. My grandmother used them for canning peaches. She'd spend the whole month of August just peeling and boiling peaches in this massive copper kettle she kept out on the back porch. The whole house would smell like sweet summer peaches for weeks. You just can't find peaches like that at the supermarket anymore, they're always so hard and mealy. I tried to make a peach cobbler last week, but the crust just didn't turn out right. I think my baking powder might be too old. But no, I haven't been saying anything rude or impulsive. I always mind my p's and q's.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any new tremors, stiffness, slowed movements, or trouble with your walking or balance recently?\n\nPatient: Trouble with my walking? Oh, no, my balance is just fine. I don't have any shaking or stiffness, nothing like that. I still walk down to the corner market every Tuesday morning to get the paper. I like to check the grocery ads, you see. Though the prices these days... well, I remember when you could get a whole pound of coffee for under a dollar. \n\nMy father drank his coffee black as tar. He boiled it right on the stove in this speckled blue enamel pot. It had a little chip on the spout where he knocked it against the sink once. He kept the coffee grounds in a big red tin, and when it was empty, he'd use the tins to hold nails and screws out in the shed. That shed always smelled like motor oil and damp earth, especially after a good rain. We had a family of raccoons get under the floorboards out there one winter. My brother tried to coax them out with pieces of crusty bread, but they just snatched the bread right out of his hand and scurried back under the wood. He was so mad about that bread.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Other medical conditions? No, no, I've never had a stroke or anything like that. My doctor says I'm pretty healthy. He did tell me to take a vitamin once, just a regular one, you know, from the pharmacy. 

The pharmacy on the corner used to have a soda fountain inside it when I was younger. Oh, it was wonderful. They had these tall, heavy glasses for ice cream sodas, with the long silver spoons. You don't see spoons like that anymore. I had one, just one, that I kept in my kitchen drawer for years. I used it to stir my iced tea in the summer. We'd sit out on the front porch and drink iced tea until the fireflies came out. Have you ever caught fireflies in a jar? We used to poke holes in the lid with an ice pick so they could breathe, but they never lasted long anyway. It always made me a little sad when their little lights went out. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he used to say the fireflies were just little stars that fell out of the sky and got lost in the tall grass. He was a nice man, always wore a straw hat when he mowed his lawn... but anyway, no, no strokes or thyroid things for me. I don't have any of those problems.

You don't see machines like that now, everything is cheap plastic and breaks if you look at it wrong. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, right. Most days now, I just sit at home. I leave the television on real low. I like watching those old game shows. Bob Barker was always such a nice-looking man in those suits, wasn't he? He always treated the ladies so polite. I know people are expecting me, the choir director keeps calling to ask where I am, but I just sit there in my chair with the TV on.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed you forgetting recent things, like appointments or conversations you had earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Forgetting things? No, I don't think so. My memory is still pretty sharp, thank the Lord. Like I said, I remember perfectly well when the choir director called. She was talking about the bake sale they're having next month. \n\nI used to bake a peach cobbler for that every single year. My sister Martha had this big old peach tree right in her backyard, and we'd go out with these woven wicker baskets to pick them. The peaches back then were just so sweet, the juice would run right down your chin. Not like the hard ones you get at the grocery store now, sitting in those plastic boxes. They don't even smell like peaches anymore, do they? Martha's yard always smelled so sweet in the late summer... she had big bushes of honeysuckle all along the fence, too. The little hummingbirds just loved that honeysuckle. We'd sit on her porch swing and just watch them zip around... it squeaked a little, that swing, but it was a nice sound.\n\nDoctor: Aside from having less energy or motivation, have you been feeling sad, down, or hopeless most days?\n\nPatient: Sad? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around crying or feeling sorry for myself, if that's what you mean. I just... sit. It's just quiet. \n\nI used to love the quiet, actually. When I was a girl, early mornings before the sun came up, that was the best time. The whole house was still asleep, and I'd go out to the porch with a cup of hot water and lemon. My grandmother swore by hot water and lemon for your digestion. She lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. She had this beautiful porcelain teacup with little blue cornflowers painted on it. I think my cousin Sarah got that cup when she passed. Sarah always did have a way of getting the nice things. She married a man who sold insurance, made a very good living. They bought a house with a wrap-around porch, just beautiful. But no, I don't feel sad. Just... not much of anything, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new difficulty managing everyday tasks—like following your quilting patterns, handling your finances, or keeping track of household chores—that used to come easily to you?\n\nPatient: Well, managing tasks... I suppose I have had to step back from some things. I used to be right up there leading the choir, you know, and helping out with all the projects down at the senior center. But lately, I just don't keep up with what I promised to do. The church friends have started checking on me a lot more than they used to... dropping by with casseroles and such. \n\nMary brought by a green bean casserole just last Tuesday. She uses those little crispy fried onions on top, from the can. I always preferred making them from scratch, though. You just slice the onions real thin, dredge them in a little flour and buttermilk, and fry them up in a cast iron skillet. My mother had this heavy skillet, black as coal, must have weighed ten pounds. She cooked everything in it—fried chicken, cornbread, even pineapple upside-down cake. The sugar would caramelize right against the iron, oh, it was just heavenly. We'd have it on Sundays after church, sitting out on the picnic table under the big oak tree. The squirrels used to come right down the trunk, trying to get a crumb... cheeky little things, they were. But yes, I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my projects like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did this drop in energy and interest come on fairly suddenly over a few weeks, or has it been creeping up slowly over a year or more?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely wasn't sudden. It's been building up slow, ever since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I used to help out in the front, you know, answering the phones and keeping the files straight. \n\nThe children were always coming in with notes from their mothers, or needing a bandage for a scraped knee from the playground. We kept a big tin of those bandages right on the counter... the ones with the little cartoon characters on them. The kids just loved them. I remember one little boy, Tommy, he'd come in almost every Tuesday just to get a bandage with a green dinosaur on it. He was a sweet little thing. His mother worked down at the hardware store, the one on Main Street that closed down last winter. It really is a shame it closed, they always had the absolute best prices on potting soil. The owner, Mr. Henderson, would even carry the bags out to my car for me. I used to buy big heavy bags of it for my geraniums. Geraniums need good drainage, you know, otherwise the roots just rot right out from under them. I had bright red ones in these terracotta pots all along the front steps...\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone else noticed you getting confused about the day or date, or losing your way in places you know well, like your own neighborhood?\n\nPatient: Confused about the day? Well, no, not really. I mean, I know it's Thursday because the trash trucks came by this morning. They always make such a racket, clanging those big metal bins around. \n\nThe driver, he's a big fellow, always wears this bright orange vest. It reminds me of the hunting vests my husband used to wear when he'd go up to the cabin with his brothers. They'd pack up the truck with coolers full of sandwiches and beer, and stay up there for the whole weekend. I never did care for the taste of venison, though. It always seemed too gamey to me. My sister-in-law, Martha, she had a recipe for venison stew that she swore by. She'd put in parsnips and carrots and let it simmer all day in the slow cooker. Martha always had the nicest kitchen gadgets. She bought one of those fancy standing mixers, the ones that come in all the pretty colors. Hers was a pale yellow, like butter. I always thought if I got one, I'd get the cherry red. It just looks so cheerful sitting on the counter. But no, I haven't been getting lost or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your family or friends mentioned that you've been repeating the same questions or stories to them without realizing you'd already said them?\n\nPatient: Repeating stories? No, I don't think my friends have said anything like that. Well, I suppose Mary did tell me I already mentioned the new curtains I was making for the spare room. \n\nI found the most beautiful floral print fabric down at the remnant store. It has these tiny little blue cornflowers all over it, with just a hint of yellow in the center. Cornflowers always remind me of my grandmother's garden. She had a whole patch of them right next to the rhubarb. She used to make the most tart rhubarb pie... you'd have to put a huge scoop of vanilla ice cream right on top just to cut the sourness. We used to get the ice cream in those big cardboard tubs from the dairy down the road. They had a big fiberglass cow out front, painted black and white. The paint was always peeling on its nose because all the kids would climb up and sit on it while their parents bought milk. I wonder if that cow is still there... they built a whole new shopping center out that way a few years back. But no, I don't think I ask the same questions over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have your family or friends mentioned any changes in your personality or behavior lately—like seeming less concerned about things that used to matter to you, or acting in ways that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Out of character? Well, I suppose the ladies down at the senior center have been looking at me a bit funny lately. They said... well, they mentioned I do some peculiar things sometimes. Like I'll just get right up in the middle of our time and start moving all the chairs around into rows, you know, setting them up just like a school classroom. \n\nWe used to have those heavy wooden desks at the school I worked at, the ones with the little groove at the top so your pencil wouldn't roll off. The janitor, Mr. Higgins, he'd polish them every single summer with this wax that smelled just like fresh lemons. I always loved the smell of lemons. I still buy those hard lemon drops at the pharmacy... the ones covered in powdered sugar that come in the little round tin. You have to pry the lid off with a butter knife sometimes because the sugar gets all stuck in the grooves. \n\nBut anyway, the ladies also say they'll catch me just folding the exact same quilt square over and over and over again. They'll be asking me to stop, saying I'm wrinkling the fabric, but I guess I just keep at it. And afterward, when they ask me about it, I can't rightly explain what I was doing or why I was doing it. I just don't know. We're making a morning star pattern quilt right now, actually. My grandmother used to make those... she'd use old feed sacks for the backing because they didn't have much money. You wouldn't believe it, but they printed the prettiest floral patterns on those feed sacks back then, just so the women could use the fabric for sewing.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for catatonic signs (e.g., prolonged immobility, mutism, waxy flexibility, or resistance to movement) to differentiate catatonia from a primary behavioral-variant dementia process\", \"question\": \"Have the ladies at the center\n\nPatient: Have the ladies at the center noticed me sitting still? Oh, like freezing up or not talking? No, they haven't mentioned anything like that. I don't think I do that. We talk plenty at the center. \n\nWe had this lovely chat yesterday about the new bakery that opened up on Main Street. Have you been there? They make these wonderful blueberry scones. The blueberries are so big and plump, not like those little shriveled things you get in the frozen aisle. My mother used to grow blueberries in the backyard. She had these huge bushes that would just hang down with the weight of them. We'd go out with these little metal pails—the kind you take to the beach to build sandcastles—and pick them until our fingers were stained purple. I always wanted a red pail, but my brother took the red one, so I had to use the blue one. He was always taking my things. He took my favorite doll once and hid it up in the hayloft. I cried for three days until my father finally climbed up there and found it. But no, I don't just sit there frozen or quiet. I'm always chatting or doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have your friends or family noticed you saying or doing things lately that seem unusually blunt, impulsive, or socially inappropriate for you—things you wouldn't have said or done in the past?\n\nPatient: Blunt or inappropriate? Oh, no, I don't think so. I've always prided myself on having good manners. My mother was a stickler for manners, you see. \n\nShe used to make us sit at the dining table with books balanced on our heads to practice our posture. If the book fell, you didn't get dessert. And dessert was always something special on Sundays, usually a tapioca pudding. I never really liked tapioca... the texture always reminded me of frog eggs. We used to go down to the creek behind the house in the spring and collect frog eggs in these big glass mason jars. The jars had those heavy zinc lids with the white glass liners. My grandmother used them for canning peaches. She'd spend the whole month of August just peeling and boiling peaches in this massive copper kettle she kept out on the back porch. The whole house would smell like sweet summer peaches for weeks. You just can't find peaches like that at the supermarket anymore, they're always so hard and mealy. I tried to make a peach cobbler last week, but the crust just didn't turn out right. I think my baking powder might be too old. But no, I haven't been saying anything rude or impulsive. I always mind my p's and q's.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any new tremors, stiffness, slowed movements, or trouble with your walking or balance recently?\n\nPatient: Trouble with my walking? Oh, no, my balance is just fine. I don't have any shaking or stiffness, nothing like that. I still walk down to the corner market every Tuesday morning to get the paper. I like to check the grocery ads, you see. Though the prices these days... well, I remember when you could get a whole pound of coffee for under a dollar. \n\nMy father drank his coffee black as tar. He boiled it right on the stove in this speckled blue enamel pot. It had a little chip on the spout where he knocked it against the sink once. He kept the coffee grounds in a big red tin, and when it was empty, he'd use the tins to hold nails and screws out in the shed. That shed always smelled like motor oil and damp earth, especially after a good rain. We had a family of raccoons get under the floorboards out there one winter. They make the strangest chittering noises, you know, almost like little birds. My brother tried to coax them out with pieces of crusty bread, but they just snatched the bread right out of his hand and scurried back under the wood. He was so mad about that bread.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other medical conditions—like a past stroke, Parkinson's disease, thyroid problems, or vitamin deficiencies—that your doctor has told you about?\n\nPatient: Other medical conditions? No, no, I've never had a stroke or anything like that. My doctor says I'm pretty healthy. He did tell me to take a vitamin once, just a regular one, you know, from the pharmacy. \n\nThe pharmacy on the corner used to have a soda fountain inside it when I was younger. Oh, it was wonderful. They had these tall, heavy glasses for ice cream sodas, with the long silver spoons. You don't see spoons like that anymore. I had one, just one, that I kept in my kitchen drawer for years. I used it to stir my iced tea in the summer. We'd sit out on the front porch and drink iced tea until the fireflies came out. Have you ever caught fireflies in a jar? We used to poke holes in the lid with an ice pick so they could breathe, but they never lasted long anyway. It always made me a little sad when their little lights went out. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he used to say the fireflies were just little stars that fell out of the sky and got lost in the tall grass. He was a nice man, always wore a straw hat when he mowed his lawn... but anyway, no, no strokes or thyroid things for me. I don't have any of those problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["G31.09"], "note": "Ruled out catatonia (no immobility/mutism), impulsivity/disinhibition, and parkinsonism/motor signs, and no secondary medical causes (stroke, thyroid, vitamin deficiency) reported, making F02.81 (dementia due to another specified condition) less likely. Findings of stereotyped, perseverative behaviors (repetitive folding, rearranging chairs), apathy, preserved memory, and gradual 2+ year progression are most consistent with behavioral-variant frontotemporal dementia.", "is_final": true}

He did tell me to take a vitamin once, just a regular one, you know, from the pharmacy. \n\nThe pharmacy on the corner used to have a soda fountain inside it when I was younger. Oh, it was wonderful. They had these tall, heavy glasses for ice cream sodas, with the long silver spoons. You don't see spoons like that anymore. I had one, just one, that I kept in my kitchen drawer for years. I used it to stir my iced tea in the summer. We'd sit out on the front porch and drink iced tea until the fireflies came out. Have you ever caught fireflies in a jar? We used to poke holes in the lid with an ice pick so they could breathe, but they never lasted long anyway. It always made me a little sad when their little lights went out. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he used to say the fireflies were just little stars that fell out of the sky and got lost in the tall grass. He was a nice man, always wore a straw hat when he mowed his lawn... but anyway, no, no strokes or thyroid things for me. I don't have any of those problems."
